### Tool call

_source line 7436 · 2026-05-23T01:43:58.463Z_

<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit · `toolu_01WPHsUiCS7g2AvPe4McAAbE`</summary>

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
  "old_string": "Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No study has simultaneously crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with up to seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B parameter instruction-tuned models and five benchmarks, totaling approximately 2.3 million cross-model evaluation records. A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 total evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ item reduction over single-condition designs (552 items, 552 evaluations). Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction). Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the second-largest component in cross-model analysis, indicating that models differ primarily in which items they find difficult. Preliminary scale validation across a 24$\\times$ parameter range (3B--72B) confirms stable variance structure within one architecture family. We observe suggestive but statistically underpowered format-associated differences in variance concentration that warrant investigation with larger benchmark samples. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.",
  "new_string": "Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a fully crossed design with up to seven facets across eight open-weight instruction-tuned models (7--9B parameters) and five benchmarks, totaling approximately 2.3 million evaluation records. D-study projections show that a recommended multi-condition design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ item reduction over single-condition designs (552 items, 552 evaluations)---trading approximately 3$\\times$ more evaluations per item for 3$\\times$ fewer items, a tradeoff that favors multi-condition designs when item construction is costly. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) indicates that models differ primarily in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. We observe suggestive but statistically underpowered format-associated differences in variance concentration between multiple-choice and free-form benchmarks. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization."
}
```

</details>

    "oldString": "    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ reduction over single-condition designs (552 items); the full 1,296-condition design reduces to 55 items (71,280 evaluations). As a sub-finding, BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring ($<$0.1\\% interaction); this separability is metric-conditional.",
    "newString": "    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ reduction over single-condition designs (552 items); the full 1,296-condition design reduces to 55 items (71,280 evaluations). Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 require no separate control ($<$0.1\\% interaction).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nIn our analysis of five benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance on multiple-choice tasks while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, the structure shifts: model$\\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for approximately 9\\%, while item selection drops to 17\\%. These numbers, drawn from a factorial analysis of approximately 2.3 million evaluation records, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No study has simultaneously crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design across benchmarks to decompose these sources, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure.\n\nExisting studies examine individual variance sources in isolation. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} documented 50--76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed up to 9\\% variation from numerical precision; \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, \\citet{dodge2020finetuning}, and \\citet{henderson2018deep} documented ordering and hyperparameter sensitivity---each holding other factors fixed. \\citet{messing2026hidden} applied G-theory to LLM evaluation across five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\\%), but excluded infrastructure facets and cross-benchmark comparison. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor metrics without crossing factors. IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt, zhou2026irt} optimize item selection but ignore prompt, seed, and ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared variance structures across response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} crossing up to seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B parameter models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, MATH), totaling approximately 2.3 million records. D-study projections show that a recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ reduction from 552 single-condition items; the full 1,296-condition design further reduces to 55 items as a theoretical ceiling (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) indicates that models differ primarily in \\emph{which items} they find difficult \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, polo2024tinybenchmarks}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig0_teaser_dstudy}\n\\caption{D-study projections for MMLU (single-model baseline). A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design reduces the budget from 552 to 178 items for $G \\geq 0.80$; the full multi-facet design reduces further to 55 items.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\\times$ reduction over single-condition designs (552 items); the full 1,296-condition design reduces to 55 items (71,280 evaluations). As a sub-finding, BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring ($<$0.1\\% interaction); this separability is metric-conditional.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\\% of variance in item-related components; FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\\%. This hypothesis-generating observation warrants confirmation with larger benchmark samples.\n\\end{enumerate}\nWe release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.",

          " ",
          " All findings in this paper are conditioned on binary correctness scoring. This measurement resolution collapses continuous generation quality into a pass/fail outcome, which may amplify item-related variance by reducing within-item response variability and mask finer-grained variance sources (e.g., partial credit, reasoning quality) that would emerge under richer scoring rubrics. The text-exact-match comparison in \\S4.1 ($G = 0.007$ vs.\\ $0.936$) illustrates how scoring metric can fundamentally reshape variance structure. A partial-credit pilot on GSM8K confirms this conditionality: $G_\\text{item}$ drops from 0.76 to 0.55, and item difficulty rankings change substantially ($\\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}), indicating that D-study budgets do not transfer to finer-grained scoring rubrics.",
          " ",
          "-Our target universe comprises open-weight, instruction-tuned models in the 7--9B parameter range (eight architecture families); results may not generalize to API-based, base, or $>$9B models. Our eight purposively selected models are treated as a quasi-random facet \\cite{meredith1993measurement, putnick2016measurement}. We report $G$ under random-model assumptions, the more conservative specification: across five benchmarks, treating models as fixed yields $G_\\text{fixed}$ of 0.951--0.990 (mean 0.975) versus $G_\\text{random}$ of 0.863--0.896 (mean 0.882), a consistent increase of 0.077--0.113 (mean 0.093), because model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\%) enters the relative error variance under random assumptions. Consequently, our D-study budgets are upper bounds---practitioners will not under-sample regardless of facet classification. The variance decomposition itself (Tables~\\ref{tab:cross_model}--\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}) is invariant to this choice. Leave-one-model-out analysis preserves component rankings across all five benchmarks, and Cohen's $\\kappa$ pairwise agreement (mean $\\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$) confirms genuine differential item functioning (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). Four-model subset forward predictions match the observed eight-model $G$ within mean $|\\Delta G| = 0.035$ across all five benchmarks (\\S4.5).",

```text
1	\section{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our eight models share the 2024--2025 instruction-tuning paradigm; temporal correlation may limit exchangeability, and results do not generalize to API-based or base models. Preliminary within-family 72B validation (\S4.6) suggests stable variance structure across scales, but is limited to a single architecture (Qwen2.5); cross-family scale validation remains future work. The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. A pilot partial-credit analysis confirms that scoring granularity fundamentally reshapes variance structure (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}); extending to continuous rubrics (F1, BLEU) is a priority for future work. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source. Reduced-crossing validation and item resampling (CV = 3.1\%) cover MMLU only. All eight models may have encountered benchmark items during pre-training; differential contamination across architectures could inflate model$\times$item interaction, though consistent variance structures across three independently constructed MC benchmarks provide indirect evidence against contamination as the sole driver \citep{golchin2023contamination}. Contamination affects absolute budget estimates but has limited impact on the multi-facet vs.\ single-condition budget \emph{ratio}, since both designs are equally exposed to contaminated items.
```

  "new_string": "Our eight models share the 2024--2025 instruction-tuning paradigm; temporal correlation may limit exchangeability, and results do not generalize to API-based or base models. Preliminary within-family 72B validation (\\S4.6) suggests stable variance structure across scales, but is limited to a single architecture (Qwen2.5); the Qwen2.5 family shares training recipes and tokenizer, so validating with architecturally distinct families (e.g., Llama-3 70B, Mistral-Large) is necessary before extrapolating budget recommendations to arbitrary model families. The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. All D-study budgets reported in this paper are strictly valid only under binary correctness scoring. Our partial-credit pilot (GSM8K only, single model) shows $G$ drops from 0.76 to 0.55 with $\\rho = 0.26$ for item rankings, indicating budgets do not transfer to finer-grained scoring rubrics; the partial-credit analysis is limited to a single benchmark and single model, and its generalizability is unknown. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source. All eight models may have encountered benchmark items during pre-training; differential contamination across architectures could inflate model$\\times$item interaction \\citep{golchin2023contamination, oren2024proving}, though consistent variance structures across three independently constructed MC benchmarks provide indirect evidence against contamination as the sole driver."

The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. A pilot partial-credit analysis confirms that scoring granularity fundamentally reshapes variance structure (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}); extending to continuous rubrics (F1, BLEU) is a priority for future work. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source. Reduced-crossing validation and item resampling (CV = 3.1\\%) cover MMLU only. All eight models may have encountered benchmark items during pre-training; differential contamination across architectures could inflate model$\\times$item interaction, though consistent variance structures across three independently constructed MC benchmarks provide indirect evidence against contamination as the sole driver \\citep{golchin2023contamination}. Contamination affects absolute budget estimates but has limited impact on the multi-facet vs.\\ single-condition budget \\emph{ratio}, since both designs are equally exposed to contaminated items.",

The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. A pilot partial-credit analysis confirms that scoring granularity fundamentally reshapes variance structure (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}); extending to continuous rubrics (F1, BLEU) is a priority for future work. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source. Reduced-crossing validation and item resampling (CV = 3.1\\%) cover MMLU only. All eight models may have encountered benchmark items during pre-training; differential contamination across architectures could inflate model$\\times$item interaction, though consistent variance structures across three independently constructed MC benchmarks provide indirect evidence against contamination as the sole driver \\citep{golchin2023contamination}. Contamination affects absolute budget estimates but has limited impact on the multi-facet vs.\\ single-condition budget \\emph{ratio}, since both designs are equally exposed to contaminated items.",
          "\\ No newline at end of file",
          "+Our eight models share the 2024--2025 instruction-tuning paradigm; temporal correlation may limit exchangeability, and results do not generalize to API-based or base models. Preliminary within-family 72B validation (\\S4.6) suggests stable variance structure across scales, but is limited to a single architecture (Qwen2.5); the Qwen2.5 family shares training recipes and tokenizer, so validating with architecturally distinct families (e.g., Llama-3 70B, Mistral-Large) is necessary before extrapolating budget recommendations to arbitrary model families. The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. All D-study budgets reported in this paper are strictly valid only under binary correctness scoring. Our partial-credit pilot (GSM8K only, single model) shows $G$ drops from 0.76 to 0.55 with $\\rho = 0.26$ for item rankings, indicating budgets do not transfer to finer-grained scoring rubrics; the partial-credit analysis is limited to a single benchmark and single model, and its generalizability is unknown. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source.           " ",
          " \\paragraph{Estimator robustness.} We compare Henderson Method~I with restricted maximum likelihood (REML-EM, 500 iterations, practical convergence $\\Delta < 0.003$ percentage points). All variance components differ by $<$0.5 percentage points between estimators. The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Probit simulation.} A probit simulation matched to our 1,296-condition design confirms that Henderson Method~I recovers variance component rankings from dichotomized data (Spearman $\\rho = 0.732$ between binary and continuous rankings, excluding residual; \\texttt{item\\_id} dominant in all 100 replications; top-5 ordering correct in 79\\% of replications). Bias analysis by difficulty stratum shows $|\\text{bias}| = 0.28$--$0.37$ for items with $p \\in [0.5, 0.8]$, where the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ is largest.           "+\\paragraph{Probit simulation.} A probit simulation matched to our 1,296-condition design confirms that Henderson Method~I recovers variance component rankings from dichotomized data (Spearman $\\rho = 0.732$ between binary and continuous rankings, excluding residual; \\texttt{item\\_id} dominant in all 100 replications; top-5 ordering correct in 79\\% of replications). Bias analysis by difficulty stratum shows $|\\text{bias}| = 0.28$--$0.37$ for items with $p \\in [0.5, 0.8]$, where the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ is largest. This analysis covers the observed accuracy range ($p \\in [0.3, 0.9]$); extreme-difficulty items ($p < 0.3$ or $p > 0.9$) are rare in our sample ($<$8\\% of items) and may exhibit larger deviations.           " ",
          " \\paragraph{CI method comparison.} Bootstrap (1,000 item-level resamples with replacement) and delete-1 jackknife confidence intervals agree closely: width ratios range from 0.96 to 1.04 across all variance components. The agreement indicates that the dependence structure introduced by item-level bootstrap resampling (which creates $\\sim$127 unique items per 200-item resample) does not materially distort interval estimates. Stratified resampling by subject (10 subjects $\\times$ 20 items) produces CIs within 4\\% of simple item-level bootstrap, confirming that the within-subject dependence structure does not materially affect interval estimates.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UVc14BpYGZkujeZYRfoRmP`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Subsequent studies applied similar decomposition to specific evaluation facets \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{messing2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\%; see Positioning below for detailed comparison). \citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Holistic evaluation frameworks \citep{liang2023helm, kiela2021dynabench} standardize multi-scenario comparison but do not decompose variance sources. \citet{blackwell2024towards} quantified uncertainty in benchmark scores, and \citet{qian2026benchmark} conducted systematic meta-evaluation of LLM benchmarks; \citet{burnell2023rethink} called for rethinking evaluation reporting standards in AI. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \citep{wei2025gtheory} and annotation reliability \citep{varannot2025gtheory}, but these studies address scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6	
7	\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}
8	A parallel line of work examines individual variance sources in isolation. \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision; \citet{messina2026background} and \citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} formalized and mitigated CUDA nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity. On the methodology side, \citet{mizrahi2024state} advocated multi-prompt evaluation based on large-scale sensitivity analysis; \citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50--76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \citet{hua2025flaw} showed that much of this sensitivity is an artifact of log-likelihood scoring (our generation-based protocol controls for this). \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, \citet{bui2025seeds}, and \citet{biderman2024lessons} documented ordering, seed, and training sensitivity respectively. Each isolates one factor, precluding interaction estimation.
9	
10	\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}
11	Item Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \citet{perlitz2023efficient} and \citet{kipnis2025metabench} explored related strategies for benchmark compression; \citet{li2025atlas} extended IRT to adaptive testing. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.
12	
13	\paragraph{Psychometrics Foundations.}
14	G-theory \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability, cardinet2010applying} provides the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis; \citet{webb2006reliability} detail the random versus fixed facet distinctions central to our model specification. Extensions to binary data include \citet{mushquash2006gtheory} and logistic-link generalized G-theory \cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}. We operationalize differential item functioning---established as a formal framework by \citet{holland1993dif} and operationalized via statistical detection procedures by \citet{clauser1998dif}---as model$\times$item interaction. Recent psychometric perspectives on LLM evaluation address construct validity \cite{kearns2026construct, bean2025measuring, freiesleben2026construct} and measurement properties \cite{ye2025psychometrics}; \citet{zhu2026benchmark} propose a benchmark health index for systematic meta-evaluation.
15	
16	\paragraph{Positioning.}
17	Closest to our work, \citet{messing2026hidden} applied G-theory to LLM evaluation through their TEE framework, decomposing five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\%). We extend this in three directions: (i) we fix the judge to the model itself, replacing judge identity with infrastructure facets and additional methodology facets (seed, ordering); (ii) we cross facets across five benchmarks, enabling cross-format comparison; and (iii) we translate variance components into reproducibility budgets via D-study projections. The two studies are complementary: TEE characterizes judge selection, while we characterize item-and-protocol selection. Relative to IRT approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory optimizes an orthogonal dimension: conditions needed for target reliability rather than item discrimination.
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}\nFactorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Subsequent studies applied similar decomposition to specific evaluation facets \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{messing2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\\%; see Positioning below for detailed comparison). \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Holistic evaluation frameworks \\citep{liang2023helm, kiela2021dynabench} standardize multi-scenario comparison but do not decompose variance sources. \\citet{blackwell2024towards} quantified uncertainty in benchmark scores, and \\citet{qian2026benchmark} conducted systematic meta-evaluation of LLM benchmarks; \\citet{burnell2023rethink} called for rethinking evaluation reporting standards; \\citet{saxon2024metrology} advocated model metrology as a framework for rigorous benchmark design. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory} and annotation reliability \\citep{varannot2025gtheory}, but these studies address scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}\nA parallel line of work examines individual variance sources in isolation. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\\% accuracy variation from numerical precision; \\citet{messina2026background} and \\citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} formalized and mitigated CUDA nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity. On the methodology side, \\citet{mizrahi2024state} advocated multi-prompt evaluation based on large-scale sensitivity analysis; \\citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50--76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \\citet{hua2025flaw} showed that much of this sensitivity is an artifact of log-likelihood scoring (our generation-based protocol controls for this). \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering}, \\citet{bui2025seeds}, and \\citet{biderman2024lessons} documented ordering, seed, and training sensitivity respectively. Each isolates one factor, precluding interaction estimation.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}\nItem Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \\citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} and \\citet{kipnis2025metabench} explored related strategies for benchmark compression; \\citet{li2025atlas} extended IRT to adaptive testing. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.\n\n\\paragraph{Psychometrics Foundations.}\nG-theory \\cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability, cardinet2010applying} provides the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis; \\citet{webb2006reliability} detail the random versus fixed facet distinctions central to our model specification. Extensions to binary data include \\citet{mushquash2006gtheory} and logistic-link generalized G-theory \\cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}. We operationalize differential item functioning---established as a formal framework by \\citet{holland1993dif} and operationalized via statistical detection procedures by \\citet{clauser1998dif}---as model$\\times$item interaction. Recent psychometric perspectives on LLM evaluation address construct validity \\cite{kearns2026construct, bean2025measuring, freiesleben2026construct} and measurement properties \\cite{ye2025psychometrics}; \\citet{zhu2026benchmark} propose a benchmark health index for systematic meta-evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nClosest to our work, \\citet{messing2026hidden} applied G-theory to LLM evaluation through their TEE framework, decomposing five methodology facets on a single benchmark (judge identity: 43.8\\%). We extend this in three directions: (i) we fix the judge to the model itself, replacing judge identity with infrastructure facets and additional methodology facets (seed, ordering); (ii) we cross facets across five benchmarks, enabling cross-format comparison; and (iii) we translate variance components into reproducibility budgets via D-study projections. The two studies are complementary: TEE characterizes judge selection, while we characterize item-and-protocol selection. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory optimizes an orthogonal dimension: conditions needed for target reliability rather than item discrimination.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019qCirrWy9iEUawYDx3Kadw`</summary>

```text
3	
4	A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces item requirements from 552 to 178 for $G \geq 0.80$, confirmed across all five benchmarks (budgets validated for 7--9B instruction-tuned models under binary scoring). The design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: multi-facet designs minimize item count when item construction is costly; single-condition designs with more items suit compute-constrained settings. All budgets are conditioned on binary scoring; partial-credit analysis confirms that finer-grained rubrics require independent calibration (\S\ref{sec:binary}). We observe suggestive but underpowered format-associated differences warranting larger benchmark samples; extending to larger models, API-based systems, and continuous metrics is a priority. Code, data, and a D-study calculator will be released upon publication.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ErNDRCMDzomiDQPdR2wckZ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces item requirements from 552 to 178 for $G \geq 0.80$, confirmed across all five benchmarks (budgets validated for 7--9B instruction-tuned models under binary scoring). The design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: multi-facet designs minimize item count when item construction is costly; single-condition designs with more items suit compute-constrained settings. All budgets are conditioned on binary scoring; partial-credit analysis confirms that finer-grained rubrics require independent calibration (\S\ref{sec:binary}). We observe suggestive but underpowered format-associated differences warranting larger benchmark samples; extending to larger models, API-based systems, and continuous metrics is a priority. Code, data, and a D-study calculator will be released upon publication.
```

</details>

All budgets are conditioned on binary scoring; partial-credit analysis confirms that finer-grained rubrics require independent calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}). We observe suggestive but underpowered format-associated differences warranting larger benchmark samples; extending to larger models, API-based systems, and continuous metrics is a priority. Code, data, and a D-study calculator will be released upon publication.",

The design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: multi-facet designs minimize item count when item construction is costly; single-condition designs with more items suit compute-constrained settings. All budgets are conditioned on binary scoring; partial-credit analysis confirms that finer-grained rubrics require independent calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}). Multi-facet designs minimize item count when item construction is costly; single-condition designs with more items suit compute-constrained settings. All budgets assume binary scoring; finer-grained rubrics require independent calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}). ```text
p.16 has about 60% whitespace — the GSM8K templates end about 40% down the page. This is the whitespace issue. I could:

1. Move the GSM8K templates to the same page as MMLU templates by making the template listing more compact
2. Use \vspace{-...} or other tricks
3. Just leave it — it's the last page of the appendix

Actually, the Director said "Visual: p.16 whitespace — Appendix 某页（约 p.16）有 95% 空白。检查 Appendix 布局，消除大面积空白（合并短 section、调整表格/图位置、或添加内容填充）。"


The whitespace is substantial but closer to 60% than 95%. Looking at the flow, page 15 contains Table 12 with the models, the prompt template description, and MMLU templates 1-6, with the last template wrapping onto page 16. I could reduce aggressive page breaks or adjust float placement, but the real issue is that the template formatting itself takes up space naturally.

Looking at the LaTeX, the enumerate lists already use minimal spacing with `\setlength{\itemsep}{2pt}`, but I could compress further by also zeroing out `\parsep` and `\topsep`. Another option is to restructure the templates into a more compact format like a tabular layout or use manual numbering with `\noindent`. I could also try `\vfill` to distribute the whitespace more evenly, though that won't eliminate it entirely.

The safest approach is probably to tighten the enumeration spacing even more—reducing `itemsep` further and removing the vertical padding around the lists.
```

For multiple-choice benchmarks, templates vary along three dimensions: option layout (lettered vs.\\ numbered), instruction wording (direct vs.\\ contextual), and answer prompt cue. For free-form benchmarks, templates vary chain-of-thought elicitation strategy. All six templates are semantically equivalent---they request the same task with different surface forms---to isolate formatting effects from content effects. 165	\label{app:difficulty_scatter}
166	
167	Figure~\ref{fig:difficulty_scatter} plots item difficulty heterogeneity ($\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) against \texttt{item\_id} variance share for all five benchmarks. MC benchmarks cluster at higher dispersion ($0.27$--$0.31$) and higher item variance ($34$--$38\%$), while FF benchmarks occupy the lower-left region ($0.22$--$0.23$, $17$--$24\%$). The cross-benchmark Spearman $\rho = 0.82$ ($p = 0.089$, $n = 5$) is consistent in direction with the within-MMLU correlation ($\rho = 0.806$, $p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects), providing multi-scale support for the item difficulty heterogeneity mechanism discussed in \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}.
168	
169	\begin{figure}[h]
170	\centering
171	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{fig4_difficulty_scatter}
172	\caption{Item difficulty heterogeneity ($\text{std}(p_i)$, computed over available models per benchmark, 6--8 models) versus \texttt{item\_id} variance share (as reported in Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}) across five benchmarks. MC benchmarks (filled) exhibit higher difficulty dispersion and item variance than FF benchmarks (open). Spearman $\rho = 0.82$, $p = 0.089$.}
173	\label{fig:difficulty_scatter}
174	\end{figure}
